========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um, my manager actually told me I needed to come in. I'm a waitress, and I've been at the same restaurant for a while now, and usually I'm really good at handling the dinner rush, you know? I even went to college for a couple of years before I started serving, so I know how to organize my thoughts and multitask. But lately... well, what really scared everyone at work—the other girls on shift, my manager, everybody—is that I just started freezing up right in the middle of service. 

Like, completely freezing. Or I'll start doing things completely out of order, which makes no sense because I know the routine backwards and forwards. Like, the other day, it was the middle of the lunch rush, and I was supposed to be closing out checks for my section. But instead of doing that, they found me just... standing over by the soda station. I was just standing there holding the cash drawer key in my hand, just gripping it, and I was just repeating table numbers out loud. Like, "table four, table nine, table twelve," just saying them over and over instead of actually closing the checks. I don't even really know how long I was standing there doing it, but my manager pulled me aside and said it really freaked people out and that I needed to get checked out before I could come back to work. Because you just can't do that when you have a section full of hungry people waiting on you, you know? So I guess that's why I'm here, to figure out why I'm getting stuck like that.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um, it's funny you ask that, because... well, yes. But it's not like I'm seeing ghosts or anything like that. It's more like... okay, so you know how when you're closing down the restaurant, it gets really quiet? Like, all the background noise from the customers and the clinking plates and the kitchen yelling, it all just stops. And it's just you and maybe one other closer and the manager in the back office. Well, sometimes when I'm doing my checkout, counting my tips and my bank, I'll hear this voice. 

It's a woman's voice, and it sounds so real, like she's standing right over my shoulder. Sometimes she just says my name, like, "Hey," or just my name, you know? But other times, it's when I'm doing the math for my checkout. I'll be counting the cash, separating the ones and the fives and the twenties, trying to make sure my drawer balances, and she'll just say, clear as day, "You're doing the count wrong." And the first few times it happened, I actually turned around and answered her! I was like, "No, I'm not, I've got it right here," and I looked behind me, and there was nobody there. The other closer was all the way across the dining room rolling silverware. 

And it's not just at work, either. It happens when I'm at home, too, usually when I'm in my own kitchen. I'll be making a sandwich or pouring a glass of water, and I'll hear her again. Just my name, or a comment about what I'm doing. And I always answer back before my brain catches up and realizes that I live alone and there's absolutely no one else in the apartment. It's so strange because it doesn't sound like a thought in my head, you know? It sounds like it's coming from outside of me, from the room. And I haven't told anyone at work about it because, I mean, after the whole freezing up thing, I don't want them to think I'm completely losing it. But it happens enough that I've started second-guessing myself when I'm doing my checkout, wondering if maybe I really am doing the count wrong, even though I know I'm good at math. I mean, I took college algebra, I know how to count a cash drawer. But when you hear someone tell you you're wrong that clearly, it really messes with your head.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... I mean, yes. I have this situation going on right now, actually, and it's driving me absolutely crazy because nobody believes me, not even my own husband. See, at the restaurant, there are these two other servers—they usually work the patio section, which is highly coveted, by the way, because that's where the big tops sit and the tips are better—and I know for an absolute fact that they are trying to get me fired. And it's not just them, they've somehow gotten one of our regular customers involved, this guy who comes in every Tuesday and Thursday for the early dinner rush. 

I know it sounds crazy to other people, but I can literally feel them watching me whenever I'm at the POS system. You know, the computer terminal where we punch in the orders? I'll be standing there, trying to type in a complicated order with a bunch of modifications, and I can feel their eyes just burning into the back of my neck, watching exactly how I ring things in. And the proof is on the tickets. The kitchen prints out these duplicate order tickets, and I started noticing these little signs on them. Like, there will be an extra space before the word "ranch," or the time stamp will be exactly one minute off from the clock on the wall, or a modification will be typed in all lowercase when the system defaults to uppercase. It's this whole subtle code they're using, leaving these little markers on the tickets to build this huge case against me, to make it look like I'm incompetent and messing up the inventory so the manager has an excuse to let me go. 

I even started bringing some of the duplicate receipts home in my apron to prove it. I laid them all out on the kitchen table for my husband last week. I was pointing right at the weird spacing and the lowercase letters, showing him exactly how it all connects back to those two servers and that regular customer, but he just looked at me like I was speaking another language. He told me I was overthinking it and said it’s just normal restaurant mess-ups, like somebody's thumb slipped on the screen or the old receipt printer in the kitchen is just glitching again. But you don't just glitch the exact same way three shifts in a row! I know what I'm seeing, and I know they're plotting against me, but because nobody else will actually look at the evidence, they all just think I'm making it up or being paranoid. But I'm not. It's really happening right in front of them.

Because you just can't do that when you have a section full of hungry people waiting on you, you know? So I guess that's why I'm here, to figure out why I'm getting stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're seeing, hearing, or believing things that other people don't seem to notice or understand?\n\nPatient: Well, um, it's funny you ask that, because... well, yes. But it's not like I'm seeing ghosts or anything like that. It's more like... okay, so you know how when you're closing down the restaurant, it gets really quiet? Like, all the background noise from the customers and the clinking plates and the kitchen yelling, it all just stops. And it's just you and maybe one other closer and the manager in the back office. Well, sometimes when I'm doing my checkout, counting my tips and my bank, I'll hear this voice. \n\nIt's a woman's voice, and it sounds so real, like she's standing right over my shoulder. Sometimes she just says my name, like, \"Hey,\" or just my name, you know? But other times, it's when I'm doing the math for my checkout. I'll be counting the cash, separating the ones and the fives and the twenties, trying to make sure my drawer balances, and she'll just say, clear as day, \"You're doing the count wrong.\" And the first few times it happened, I actually turned around and answered her! I was like, \"No, I'm not, I've got it right here,\" and I looked behind me, and there was nobody there. The other closer was all the way across the dining room rolling silverware. \n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. It happens when I'm at home, too, usually when I'm in my own kitchen. I'll be making a sandwich or pouring a glass of water, and I'll hear her again. Just my name, or a comment about what I'm doing. And I always answer back before my brain catches up and realizes that I live alone and there's absolutely no one else in the apartment. It's so strange because it doesn't sound like a thought in my head, you know? It sounds like it's coming from outside of me, from the room. And I haven't told anyone at work about it because, I mean, after the whole freezing up thing, I don't want them to think I'm completely losing it. But it happens enough that I've started second-guessing myself when I'm doing my checkout, wondering if maybe I really am doing the count wrong, even though I know I'm good at math. I mean, I took college algebra, I know how to count a cash drawer. But when you hear someone tell you you're wrong that clearly, it really messes with your head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts or beliefs that you know are completely true, but you realize most other people would say they aren't real or don't make sense?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I mean, yes. I have this situation going on right now, actually, and it's driving me absolutely crazy because nobody believes me, not even my own husband. See, at the restaurant, there are these two other servers—they usually work the patio section, which is highly coveted, by the way, because that's where the big tops sit and the tips are better—and I know for an absolute fact that they are trying to get me fired. And it's not just them, they've somehow gotten one of our regular customers involved, this guy who comes in every Tuesday and Thursday for the early dinner rush. \n\nI know it sounds crazy to other people, but I can literally feel them watching me whenever I'm at the POS system. You know, the computer terminal where we punch in the orders? I'll be standing there, trying to type in a complicated order with a bunch of modifications, and I can feel their eyes just burning into the back of my neck, watching exactly how I ring things in. And the proof is on the tickets. The kitchen prints out these duplicate order tickets, and I started noticing these little signs on them. Like, there will be an extra space before the word \"ranch,\" or the time stamp will be exactly one minute off from the clock on the wall, or a modification will be typed in all lowercase when the system defaults to uppercase. It's this whole subtle code they're using, leaving these little markers on the tickets to build this huge case against me, to make it look like I'm incompetent and messing up the inventory so the manager has an excuse to let me go. \n\nI even started bringing some of the duplicate receipts home in my apron to prove it. I laid them all out on the kitchen table for my husband last week. I was pointing right at the weird spacing and the lowercase letters, showing him exactly how it all connects back to those two servers and that regular customer, but he just looked at me like I was speaking another language. He told me I was overthinking it and said it’s just normal restaurant mess-ups, like somebody's thumb slipped on the screen or the old receipt printer in the kitchen is just glitching again. But you don't just glitch the exact same way three shifts in a row! I know what I'm seeing, and I know they're plotting against me, but because nobody else will actually look at the evidence, they all just think I'm making it up or being paranoid. But I'm not. It's really happening right in front of them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

But you don't just glitch the exact same way three shifts in a row! I know what I'm seeing, and I know they're plotting against me, but because nobody else will actually look at the evidence, they all just think I'm making it up or being paranoid. But I'm not. It's really happening right in front of them.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had these beliefs about people trying to get you fired and the signs on the order tickets for at least six months now?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\nmanifestation: \"The clearer voices and firm beliefs about coworkers and customers targeting me have been happening for about four months.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Six months? Um, no, it hasn't been quite that long. Let me think about it, because, you know, in the restaurant industry, time just kind of blurs together. You're just living shift to shift, lunch rush to dinner rush, and sometimes you don't even know what day of the week it is unless you look at the specials board. 

But if I really sit down and trace it back... the really clear stuff, like hearing that woman's voice so plainly when I'm doing my bank at night, and having those firm, undeniable beliefs about the patio servers and that regular customer targeting me... that really all solidified about four months ago. Yeah, it would be about four months. Because I remember it was right around the time we did the big seasonal menu rollout, which is always a complete nightmare anyway because they update the POS system and everyone is trying to memorize the new sides and modifiers. That's when I really started catching on to what they were doing with the tickets and the spacing. 

Before that, I mean, I was stressed out, sure, but the actual clearer voices and the absolute certainty that they are coordinating this whole thing to get me fired? That's been going on for a solid four months now. And let me tell you, four months feels like an absolute eternity when you have to go into work every single day on high alert. I spend my whole shift just watching my back, checking every single duplicate receipt that prints out of the kitchen, making sure my cash drawer is absolutely perfect so they can't use it against me, and it's just exhausting. Four months of that kind of stress will really wear a person down, you know? Because you can't just clock out and leave it behind when you know they're probably texting each other about you the second you walk out the door.

Like, the other day, it was the middle of the lunch rush, and I was supposed to be closing out checks for my section. I was just standing there holding the cash drawer key in my hand, just gripping it, and I was just repeating table numbers out loud. Like, \"table four, table nine, table twelve,\" just saying them over and over instead of actually closing the checks. I don't even really know how long I was standing there doing it, but my manager pulled me aside and said it really freaked people out and that I needed to get checked out before I could come back to work. Because you just can't do that when you have a section full of hungry people waiting on you, you know? So I guess that's why I'm here, to figure out why I'm getting stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're seeing, hearing, or believing things that other people don't seem to notice or understand?\n\nPatient: Well, um, it's funny you ask that, because... well, yes. But it's not like I'm seeing ghosts or anything like that. It's more like... okay, so you know how when you're closing down the restaurant, it gets really quiet? Like, all the background noise from the customers and the clinking plates and the kitchen yelling, it all just stops. And it's just you and maybe one other closer and the manager in the back office. Well, sometimes when I'm doing my checkout, counting my tips and my bank, I'll hear this voice. \n\nIt's a woman's voice, and it sounds so real, like she's standing right over my shoulder. Sometimes she just says my name, like, \"Hey,\" or just my name, you know? But other times, it's when I'm doing the math for my checkout. I'll be counting the cash, separating the ones and the fives and the twenties, trying to make sure my drawer balances, and she'll just say, clear as day, \"You're doing the count wrong.\" And the first few times it happened, I actually turned around and answered her! I was like, \"No, I'm not, I've got it right here,\" and I looked behind me, and there was nobody there. The other closer was all the way across the dining room rolling silverware. \n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. It happens when I'm at home, too, usually when I'm in my own kitchen. I'll be making a sandwich or pouring a glass of water, and I'll hear her again. Just my name, or a comment about what I'm doing. And I always answer back before my brain catches up and realizes that I live alone and there's absolutely no one else in the apartment. It's so strange because it doesn't sound like a thought in my head, you know? It sounds like it's coming from outside of me, from the room. And I haven't told anyone at work about it because, I mean, after the whole freezing up thing, I don't want them to think I'm completely losing it. But it happens enough that I've started second-guessing myself when I'm doing my checkout, wondering if maybe I really am doing the count wrong, even though I know I'm good at math. I mean, I took college algebra, I know how to count a cash drawer. But when you hear someone tell you you're wrong that clearly, it really messes with your head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts or beliefs that you know are completely true, but you realize most other people would say they aren't real or don't make sense?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I mean, yes. I have this situation going on right now, actually, and it's driving me absolutely crazy because nobody believes me, not even my own husband. See, at the restaurant, there are these two other servers—they usually work the patio section, which is highly coveted, by the way, because that's where the big tops sit and the tips are better—and I know for an absolute fact that they are trying to get me fired. And it's not just them, they've somehow gotten one of our regular customers involved, this guy who comes in every Tuesday and Thursday for the early dinner rush. \n\nI know it sounds crazy to other people, but I can literally feel them watching me whenever I'm at the POS system. You know, the computer terminal where we punch in the orders? I'll be standing there, trying to type in a complicated order with a bunch of modifications, and I can feel their eyes just burning into the back of my neck, watching exactly how I ring things in. Like, there will be an extra space before the word \"ranch,\" or the time stamp will be exactly one minute off from the clock on the wall, or a modification will be typed in all lowercase when the system defaults to uppercase. It's this whole subtle code they're using, leaving these little markers on the tickets to build this huge case against me, to make it look like I'm incompetent and messing up the inventory so the manager has an excuse to let me go. \n\nI even started bringing some of the duplicate receipts home in my apron to prove it. I laid them all out on the kitchen table for my husband last week. I was pointing right at the weird spacing and the lowercase letters, showing him exactly how it all connects back to those two servers and that regular customer, but he just looked at me like I was speaking another language. He told me I was overthinking it and said it’s just normal restaurant mess-ups, like somebody's thumb slipped on the screen or the old receipt printer in the kitchen is just glitching again. But you don't just glitch the exact same way three shifts in a row! I know what I'm seeing, and I know they're plotting against me, but because nobody else will actually look at the evidence, they all just think I'm making it up or being paranoid. But I'm not. I was pointing right at the weird spacing and the lowercase letters, showing him exactly how it all connects back to those two servers and that regular customer, but he just looked at me like I was speaking another language. He told me I was overthinking it and said it’s just normal restaurant mess-ups, like somebody's thumb slipped on the screen or the old receipt printer in the kitchen is just glitching again. But you don't just glitch the exact same way three shifts in a row! I know what I'm seeing, and I know they're plotting against me, but because nobody else will actually look at the evidence, they all just think I'm making it up or being paranoid. But I'm not. It's really happening right in front of them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about people trying to get you fired and the signs on the order tickets for at least six months now?\n\nPatient: Six months? Um, no, it hasn't been quite that long. Let me think about it, because, you know, in the restaurant industry, time just kind of blurs together. You're just living shift to shift, lunch rush to dinner rush, and sometimes you don't even know what day of the week it is unless you look at the specials board. \n\nBut if I really sit down and trace it back... the really clear stuff, like hearing that woman's voice so plainly when I'm doing my bank at night, and having those firm, undeniable beliefs about the patio servers and that regular customer targeting me... that really all solidified about four months ago. Yeah, it would be about four months. Because I remember it was right around the time we did the big seasonal menu rollout, which is always a complete nightmare anyway because they update the POS system and everyone is trying to memorize the new sides and modifiers. That's when I really started catching on to what they were doing with the tickets and the spacing. \n\nBefore that, I mean, I was stressed out, sure, but the actual clearer voices and the absolute certainty that they are coordinating this whole thing to get me fired? That's been going on for a solid four months now. And let me tell you, four months feels like an absolute eternity when you have to go into work every single day on high alert. I spend my whole shift just watching my back, checking every single duplicate receipt that prints out of the kitchen, making sure my cash drawer is absolutely perfect so they can't use it against me, and it's just exhausting. Four months of that kind of stress will really wear a person down, you know? Because you can't just clock out and leave it behind when you know they're probably texting each other about you the second you walk out the door.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any signs or symptoms like these—such as hearing voices or feeling like people are plotting against you—for at least six months now?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, if you mean just the start of it all... like, any signs that something was off or that things were starting to shift, then yes, definitely longer than six months. Like I said before, the really intense stuff, the clear voice and catching them using the ticket codes, that was four months ago. But if I really think about it, this whole change in me has been building for about a year and two months. 

It started exactly a year and two months ago, because that is when the general manager pulled me aside before a busy Friday night and asked me to take on the evening shift lead responsibilities. Which, I mean, it sounds like a good thing, like a promotion, right? But in the restaurant industry, being a shift lead just means you do all the manager work for barely any extra hourly pay. You have to handle the voids, the comps, deal with angry tables when the kitchen overcooks a steak, and you have to make sure every single server rolls their silverware and does their sidework before they are allowed to get cut and go home. 

And that is when everything really started shifting. I remember feeling this massive change in the atmosphere almost immediately. I would walk into the waitstation to grab a stack of napkins or restock the straws, and it felt like everyone had just stopped whispering about me the second the door swung open. I started feeling this heavy pressure, like all eyes were on me, just waiting for me to fail at being the lead. I was so stressed out about balancing the master drawer and dealing with the nightly deposits that I just felt my whole personality changing. I was on edge all the time, always looking over my shoulder, always wondering what those patio servers were saying behind my back because they were mad they did not get the lead position. 

So the really loud, undeniable stuff with the receipts and the voice telling me my math is wrong, that is more recent. But the actual beginning of this whole change, this feeling that things were wrong and people were against me? That has been building up for a year and two months, ever since I got those shift lead keys. It is just that nobody warns you how much a tiny bit of authority is going to completely ruin your life and make everyone turn on you, and it just slowly built up and built up until we got to where we are now.

And I haven't told anyone at work about it because, I mean, after the whole freezing up thing, I don't want them to think I'm completely losing it. But it happens enough that I've started second-guessing myself when I'm doing my checkout, wondering if maybe I really am doing the count wrong, even though I know I'm good at math. I mean, I took college algebra, I know how to count a cash drawer. But when you hear someone tell you you're wrong that clearly, it really messes with your head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts or beliefs that you know are completely true, but you realize most other people would say they aren't real or don't make sense?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I mean, yes. I have this situation going on right now, actually, and it's driving me absolutely crazy because nobody believes me, not even my own husband. See, at the restaurant, there are these two other servers—they usually work the patio section, which is highly coveted, by the way, because that's where the big tops sit and the tips are better—and I know for an absolute fact that they are trying to get me fired. And it's not just them, they've somehow gotten one of our regular customers involved, this guy who comes in every Tuesday and Thursday for the early dinner rush. \n\nI know it sounds crazy to other people, but I can literally feel them watching me whenever I'm at the POS system. You know, the computer terminal where we punch in the orders? I'll be standing there, trying to type in a complicated order with a bunch of modifications, and I can feel their eyes just burning into the back of my neck, watching exactly how I ring things in. And the proof is on the tickets. The kitchen prints out these duplicate order tickets, and I started noticing these little signs on them. Like, there will be an extra space before the word \"ranch,\" or the time stamp will be exactly one minute off from the clock on the wall, or a modification will be typed in all lowercase when the system defaults to uppercase. It's this whole subtle code they're using, leaving these little markers on the tickets to build this huge case against me, to make it look like I'm incompetent and messing up the inventory so the manager has an excuse to let me go. \n\nI even started bringing some of the duplicate receipts home in my apron to prove it. I laid them all out on the kitchen table for my husband last week. I was pointing right at the weird spacing and the lowercase letters, showing him exactly how it all connects back to those two servers and that regular customer, but he just looked at me like I was speaking another language. He told me I was overthinking it and said it’s just normal restaurant mess-ups, like somebody's thumb slipped on the screen or the old receipt printer in the kitchen is just glitching again. But you don't just glitch the exact same way three shifts in a row! I know what I'm seeing, and I know they're plotting against me, but because nobody else will actually look at the evidence, they all just think I'm making it up or being paranoid. But I'm not. It's really happening right in front of them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about people trying to get you fired and the signs on the order tickets for at least six months now?\n\nPatient: Six months? Um, no, it hasn't been quite that long. Let me think about it, because, you know, in the restaurant industry, time just kind of blurs together. You're just living shift to shift, lunch rush to dinner rush, and sometimes you don't even know what day of the week it is unless you look at the specials board. \n\nBut if I really sit down and trace it back... the really clear stuff, like hearing that woman's voice so plainly when I'm doing my bank at night, and having those firm, undeniable beliefs about the patio servers and that regular customer targeting me... that really all solidified about four months ago. Yeah, it would be about four months. Because I remember it was right around the time we did the big seasonal menu rollout, which is always a complete nightmare anyway because they update the POS system and everyone is trying to memorize the new sides and modifiers. That's when I really started catching on to what they were doing with the tickets and the spacing. \n\nBefore that, I mean, I was stressed out, sure, but the actual clearer voices and the absolute certainty that they are coordinating this whole thing to get me fired? That's been going on for a solid four months now. And let me tell you, four months feels like an absolute eternity when you have to go into work every single day on high alert. I spend my whole shift just watching my back, checking every single duplicate receipt that prints out of the kitchen, making sure my cash drawer is absolutely perfect so they can't use it against me, and it's just exhausting. Four months of that kind of stress will really wear a person down, you know? Because you can't just clock out and leave it behind when you know they're probably texting each other about you the second you walk out the door.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any signs or symptoms like these—such as hearing voices or feeling like people are plotting against you—for at least six months now?\n\nPatient: Well, if you mean just the start of it all... like, any signs that something was off or that things were starting to shift, then yes, definitely longer than six months. Like I said before, the really intense stuff, the clear voice and catching them using the ticket codes, that was four months ago. But if I really think about it, this whole change in me has been building for about a year and two months. \n\nIt started exactly a year and two months ago, because that is when the general manager pulled me aside before a busy Friday night and asked me to take on the evening shift lead responsibilities. Which, I mean, it sounds like a good thing, like a promotion, right? But in the restaurant industry, being a shift lead just means you do all the manager work for barely any extra hourly pay. You have to handle the voids, the comps, deal with angry tables when the kitchen overcooks a steak, and you have to make sure every single server rolls their silverware and does their sidework before they are allowed to get cut and go home. \n\nAnd that is when everything really started shifting. I remember feeling this massive change in the atmosphere almost immediately. I would walk into the waitstation to grab a stack of napkins or restock the straws, and it felt like everyone had just stopped whispering about me the second the door swung open. I started feeling this heavy pressure, like all eyes were on me, just waiting for me to fail at being the lead. I was so stressed out about balancing the master drawer and dealing with the nightly deposits that I just felt my whole personality changing. I was on edge all the time, always looking over my shoulder, always wondering what those patio servers were saying behind my back because they were mad they did not get the lead position. \n\nSo the really loud, undeniable stuff with the receipts and the voice telling me my math is wrong, that is more recent. But the actual beginning of this whole change, this feeling that things were wrong and people were against me? That has been building up for a year and two months, ever since I got those shift lead keys. It is just that nobody warns you how much a tiny bit of authority is going to completely ruin your life and make everyone turn on you, and it just slowly built up and built up until we got to where we are now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it’s... it’s not every single second of the day, but it definitely follows me home. It’s not just when I’m at the restaurant or staring at the POS system. Like, you asked if it happens outside of work, and it absolutely does. 

Sometimes it happens during closing, when I’m in the back office with that awful flickering fluorescent light that always gives me a migraine, just trying to finish up the deposit so I can finally leave. But then other times, I’ll literally just be at home, in my own kitchen. I’ll just be standing there, maybe making a cup of chamomile tea to try and unwind after a brutal ten-hour shift on my feet, or just organizing the mail on the counter because my husband just lets it pile up for days if I don't deal with it, and out of nowhere, I hear her. 

It’s a woman’s voice. And it is so, so incredibly clear. She’ll just say my name, right out loud, or she’ll tell me I’m doing the count wrong. Which is so strange because half the time at home I’m not even counting anything, I'm just trying to relax, but she says it anyway. And it sounds so real, so much like a physical person standing directly behind my left shoulder, that I actually answer her out loud. I’ll turn around and say, "What do you want?" or "No, I'm doing it right," and then... I just freeze. Because I’ll realize the house is completely quiet, my husband is in the other room watching TV, and there is absolutely nobody else in the kitchen with me. 

I'll just be standing there holding a mug or a stack of envelopes, realizing nobody else heard it. But I mean, how can nobody else hear it when it’s loud enough for me to reflexively answer back? It just makes my heart pound out of my chest. It’s exhausting enough trying to keep track of the patio servers and the duplicate tickets at work, but to come home and have this woman's voice telling me I'm messing up my count while I'm standing in my own kitchen... it just never really stops, you know? You never really get to just turn your brain off and rest.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, um, my manager actually told me I needed to come in. I'm a waitress, and I've been at the same restaurant for a while now, and usually I'm really good at handling the dinner rush, you know? I even went to college for a couple of years before I started serving, so I know how to organize my thoughts and multitask. But lately... well, what really scared everyone at work—the other girls on shift, my manager, everybody—is that I just started freezing up right in the middle of service. \n\nLike, completely freezing. Or I'll start doing things completely out of order, which makes no sense because I know the routine backwards and forwards. Like, the other day, it was the middle of the lunch rush, and I was supposed to be closing out checks for my section. But instead of doing that, they found me just... standing over by the soda station. I was just standing there holding the cash drawer key in my hand, just gripping it, and I was just repeating table numbers out loud. Like, \"table four, table nine, table twelve,\" just saying them over and over instead of actually closing the checks. I don't even really know how long I was standing there doing it, but my manager pulled me aside and said it really freaked people out and that I needed to get checked out before I could come back to work. Because you just can't do that when you have a section full of hungry people waiting on you, you know? So I guess that's why I'm here, to figure out why I'm getting stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're seeing, hearing, or believing things that other people don't seem to notice or understand?\n\nPatient: Well, um, it's funny you ask that, because... well, yes. But it's not like I'm seeing ghosts or anything like that. It's more like... okay, so you know how when you're closing down the restaurant, it gets really quiet? Like, all the background noise from the customers and the clinking plates and the kitchen yelling, it all just stops. And it's just you and maybe one other closer and the manager in the back office. Well, sometimes when I'm doing my checkout, counting my tips and my bank, I'll hear this voice. \n\nIt's a woman's voice, and it sounds so real, like she's standing right over my shoulder. Sometimes she just says my name, like, \"Hey,\" or just my name, you know? But other times, it's when I'm doing the math for my checkout. I'll be counting the cash, separating the ones and the fives and the twenties, trying to make sure my drawer balances, and she'll just say, clear as day, \"You're doing the count wrong.\" And the first few times it happened, I actually turned around and answered her! I was like, \"No, I'm not, I've got it right here,\" and I looked behind me, and there was nobody there. The other closer was all the way across the dining room rolling silverware. \n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. It happens when I'm at home, too, usually when I'm in my own kitchen. I'll be making a sandwich or pouring a glass of water, and I'll hear her again. Just my name, or a comment about what I'm doing. And I always answer back before my brain catches up and realizes that I live alone and there's absolutely no one else in the apartment. It's so strange because it doesn't sound like a thought in my head, you know? It sounds like it's coming from outside of me, from the room. And I haven't told anyone at work about it because, I mean, after the whole freezing up thing, I don't want them to think I'm completely losing it. But it happens enough that I've started second-guessing myself when I'm doing my checkout, wondering if maybe I really am doing the count wrong, even though I know I'm good at math. I mean, I took college algebra, I know how to count a cash drawer. But when you hear someone tell you you're wrong that clearly, it really messes with your head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts or beliefs that you know are completely true, but you realize most other people would say they aren't real or don't make sense?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I mean, yes. I have this situation going on right now, actually, and it's driving me absolutely crazy because nobody believes me, not even my own husband. See, at the restaurant, there are these two other servers—they usually work the patio section, which is highly coveted, by the way, because that's where the big tops sit and the tips are better—and I know for an absolute fact that they are trying to get me fired. And it's not just them, they've somehow gotten one of our regular customers involved, this guy who comes in every Tuesday and Thursday for the early dinner rush. \n\nI know it sounds crazy to other people, but I can literally feel them watching me whenever I'm at the POS system. You know, the computer terminal where we punch in the orders? I'll be standing there, trying to type in a complicated order with a bunch of modifications, and I can feel their eyes just burning into the back of my neck, watching exactly how I ring things in. And the proof is on the tickets. The kitchen prints out these duplicate order tickets, and I started noticing these little signs on them. Like, there will be an extra space before the word \"ranch,\" or the time stamp will be exactly one minute off from the clock on the wall, or a modification will be typed in all lowercase when the system defaults to uppercase. It's this whole subtle code they're using, leaving these little markers on the tickets to build this huge case against me, to make it look like I'm incompetent and messing up the inventory so the manager has an excuse to let me go. \n\nI even started bringing some of the duplicate receipts home in my apron to prove it. I laid them all out on the kitchen table for my husband last week. I was pointing right at the weird spacing and the lowercase letters, showing him exactly how it all connects back to those two servers and that regular customer, but he just looked at me like I was speaking another language. He told me I was overthinking it and said it’s just normal restaurant mess-ups, like somebody's thumb slipped on the screen or the old receipt printer in the kitchen is just glitching again. But you don't just glitch the exact same way three shifts in a row! I know what I'm seeing, and I know they're plotting against me, but because nobody else will actually look at the evidence, they all just think I'm making it up or being paranoid. But I'm not. It's really happening right in front of them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about people trying to get you fired and the signs on the order tickets for at least six months now?\n\nPatient: Six months? Um, no, it hasn't been quite that long. Let me think about it, because, you know, in the restaurant industry, time just kind of blurs together. You're just living shift to shift, lunch rush to dinner rush, and sometimes you don't even know what day of the week it is unless you look at the specials board. \n\nBut if I really sit down and trace it back... the really clear stuff, like hearing that woman's voice so plainly when I'm doing my bank at night, and having those firm, undeniable beliefs about the patio servers and that regular customer targeting me... that really all solidified about four months ago. Yeah, it would be about four months. Because I remember it was right around the time we did the big seasonal menu rollout, which is always a complete nightmare anyway because they update the POS system and everyone is trying to memorize the new sides and modifiers. That's when I really started catching on to what they were doing with the tickets and the spacing. \n\nBefore that, I mean, I was stressed out, sure, but the actual clearer voices and the absolute certainty that they are coordinating this whole thing to get me fired? That's been going on for a solid four months now. And let me tell you, four months feels like an absolute eternity when you have to go into work every single day on high alert. I spend my whole shift just watching my back, checking every single duplicate receipt that prints out of the kitchen, making sure my cash drawer is absolutely perfect so they can't use it against me, and it's just exhausting. Four months of that kind of stress will really wear a person down, you know? Because you can't just clock out and leave it behind when you know they're probably texting each other about you the second you walk out the door.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any signs or symptoms like these—such as hearing voices or feeling like people are plotting against you—for at least six months now?\n\nPatient: Well, if you mean just the start of it all... like, any signs that something was off or that things were starting to shift, then yes, definitely longer than six months. Like I said before, the really intense stuff, the clear voice and catching them using the ticket codes, that was four months ago. But if I really think about it, this whole change in me has been building for about a year and two months. \n\nIt started exactly a year and two months ago, because that is when the general manager pulled me aside before a busy Friday night and asked me to take on the evening shift lead responsibilities. Which, I mean, it sounds like a good thing, like a promotion, right? But in the restaurant industry, being a shift lead just means you do all the manager work for barely any extra hourly pay. You have to handle the voids, the comps, deal with angry tables when the kitchen overcooks a steak, and you have to make sure every single server rolls their silverware and does their sidework before they are allowed to get cut and go home. \n\nAnd that is when everything really started shifting. I remember feeling this massive change in the atmosphere almost immediately. I would walk into the waitstation to grab a stack of napkins or restock the straws, and it felt like everyone had just stopped whispering about me the second the door swung open. I started feeling this heavy pressure, like all eyes were on me, just waiting for me to fail at being the lead. I was so stressed out about balancing the master drawer and dealing with the nightly deposits that I just felt my whole personality changing. I was on edge all the time, always looking over my shoulder, always wondering what those patio servers were saying behind my back because they were mad they did not get the lead position. \n\nSo the really loud, undeniable stuff with the receipts and the voice telling me my math is wrong, that is more recent. But the actual beginning of this whole change, this feeling that things were wrong and people were against me? That has been building up for a year and two months, ever since I got those shift lead keys. It is just that nobody warns you how much a tiny bit of authority is going to completely ruin your life and make everyone turn on you, and it just slowly built up and built up until we got to where we are now.\n\nDoctor: Are these experiences—like hearing the voice or feeling like people are plotting against you—something you notice all the time, even when you're not at work or doing tasks like counting your cash drawer?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s not every single second of the day, but it definitely follows me home. It’s not just when I’m at the restaurant or staring at the POS system. Like, you asked if it happens outside of work, and it absolutely does. \n\nSometimes it happens during closing, when I’m in the back office with that awful flickering fluorescent light that always gives me a migraine, just trying to finish up the deposit so I can finally leave. But then other times, I’ll literally just be at home, in my own kitchen. I’ll just be standing there, maybe making a cup of chamomile tea to try and unwind after a brutal ten-hour shift on my feet, or just organizing the mail on the counter because my husband just lets it pile up for days if I don't deal with it, and out of nowhere, I hear her. \n\nIt’s a woman’s voice. And it is so, so incredibly clear. She’ll just say my name, right out loud, or she’ll tell me I’m doing the count wrong. Which is so strange because half the time at home I’m not even counting anything, I'm just trying to relax, but she says it anyway. And it sounds so real, so much like a physical person standing directly behind my left shoulder, that I actually answer her out loud. I’ll turn around and say, \"What do you want?\" or \"No, I'm doing it right,\" and then... I just freeze. Because I’ll realize the house is completely quiet, my husband is in the other room watching TV, and there is absolutely nobody else in the kitchen with me. \n\nI'll just be standing there holding a mug or a stack of envelopes, realizing nobody else heard it. But I mean, how can nobody else hear it when it’s loud enough for me to reflexively answer back? It just makes my heart pound out of my chest. It’s exhausting enough trying to keep track of the patio servers and the duplicate tickets at work, but to come home and have this woman's voice telling me I'm messing up my count while I'm standing in my own kitchen... it just never really stops, you know? You never really get to just turn your brain off and rest.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I spend my whole shift just watching my back, checking every single duplicate receipt that prints out of the kitchen, making sure my cash drawer is absolutely perfect so they can't use it against me, and it's just exhausting. Four months of that kind of stress will really wear a person down, you know? Because you can't just clock out and leave it behind when you know they're probably texting each other about you the second you walk out the door.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any signs or symptoms like these—such as hearing voices or feeling like people are plotting against you—for at least six months now?\n\nPatient: Well, if you mean just the start of it all... like, any signs that something was off or that things were starting to shift, then yes, definitely longer than six months. Like I said before, the really intense stuff, the clear voice and catching them using the ticket codes, that was four months ago. But if I really think about it, this whole change in me has been building for about a year and two months. \n\nIt started exactly a year and two months ago, because that is when the general manager pulled me aside before a busy Friday night and asked me to take on the evening shift lead responsibilities. Which, I mean, it sounds like a good thing, like a promotion, right? But in the restaurant industry, being a shift lead just means you do all the manager work for barely any extra hourly pay. You have to handle the voids, the comps, deal with angry tables when the kitchen overcooks a steak, and you have to make sure every single server rolls their silverware and does their sidework before they are allowed to get cut and go home. \n\nAnd that is when everything really started shifting. I remember feeling this massive change in the atmosphere almost immediately. I would walk into the waitstation to grab a stack of napkins or restock the straws, and it felt like everyone had just stopped whispering about me the second the door swung open. I started feeling this heavy pressure, like all eyes were on me, just waiting for me to fail at being the lead. I was so stressed out about balancing the master drawer and dealing with the nightly deposits that I just felt my whole personality changing. I was on edge all the time, always looking over my shoulder, always wondering what those patio servers were saying behind my back because they were mad they did not get the lead position. \n\nSo the really loud, undeniable stuff with the receipts and the voice telling me my math is wrong, that is more recent. But the actual beginning of this whole change, this feeling that things were wrong and people were against me? That has been building up for a year and two months, ever since I got those shift lead keys. It is just that nobody warns you how much a tiny bit of authority is going to completely ruin your life and make everyone turn on you, and it just slowly built up and built up until we got to where we are now.\n\nDoctor: Are these experiences—like hearing the voice or feeling like people are plotting against you—something you notice all the time, even when you're not at work or doing tasks like counting your cash drawer?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s not every single second of the day, but it definitely follows me home. It’s not just when I’m at the restaurant or staring at the POS system. Like, you asked if it happens outside of work, and it absolutely does. \n\nSometimes it happens during closing, when I’m in the back office with that awful flickering fluorescent light that always gives me a migraine, just trying to finish up the deposit so I can finally leave. But then other times, I’ll literally just be at home, in my own kitchen. I’ll just be standing there, maybe making a cup of chamomile tea to try and unwind after a brutal ten-hour shift on my feet, or just organizing the mail on the counter because my husband just lets it pile up for days if I don't deal with it, and out of nowhere, I hear her. \n\nIt’s a woman’s voice. And it is so, so incredibly clear. She’ll just say my name, right out loud, or she’ll tell me I’m doing the count wrong. Which is so strange because half the time at home I’m not even counting anything, I'm just trying to relax, but she says it anyway. And it sounds so real, so much like a physical person standing directly behind my left shoulder, that I actually answer her out loud. I’ll turn around and say, \"What do you want?\" or \"No, I'm doing it right,\" and then... I just freeze. Because I’ll realize the house is completely quiet, my husband is in the other room watching TV, and there is absolutely nobody else in the kitchen with me. \n\nI'll just be standing there holding a mug or a stack of envelopes, realizing nobody else heard it. But I mean, how can nobody else hear it when it’s loud enough for me to reflexively answer back? It just makes my heart pound out of my chest. It’s exhausting enough trying to keep track of the patio servers and the duplicate tickets at work, but to come home and have this woman's voice telling me I'm messing up my count while I'm standing in my own kitchen... it just never really stops, you know? You never really get to just turn your brain off and rest.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think back and tell me if there was ever a time when you experienced these kinds of things—like hearing the voice or believing others were plotting against you—for at least one month straight without a break?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, if you mean for a solid month without a single break... yeah, absolutely. And the voice... she doesn't take a day off either. Like I told you, she's there when I'm just trying to make tea in my own kitchen. You get so tired of always being on high alert, always waiting for the other shoe to drop, always waiting for them to finally make their move and get me fired. It's been four months of this, every single day, and I just... I don't know how much longer I can keep this up before I just completely break down in the middle of a dinner rush.

It is just that nobody warns you how much a tiny bit of authority is going to completely ruin your life and make everyone turn on you, and it just slowly built up and built up until we got to where we are now.\n\nDoctor: Are these experiences—like hearing the voice or feeling like people are plotting against you—something you notice all the time, even when you're not at work or doing tasks like counting your cash drawer?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s... it’s not every single second of the day, but it definitely follows me home. It’s not just when I’m at the restaurant or staring at the POS system. Like, you asked if it happens outside of work, and it absolutely does. \n\nSometimes it happens during closing, when I’m in the back office with that awful flickering fluorescent light that always gives me a migraine, just trying to finish up the deposit so I can finally leave. But then other times, I’ll literally just be at home, in my own kitchen. I’ll just be standing there, maybe making a cup of chamomile tea to try and unwind after a brutal ten-hour shift on my feet, or just organizing the mail on the counter because my husband just lets it pile up for days if I don't deal with it, and out of nowhere, I hear her. \n\nIt’s a woman’s voice. And it is so, so incredibly clear. She’ll just say my name, right out loud, or she’ll tell me I’m doing the count wrong. Which is so strange because half the time at home I’m not even counting anything, I'm just trying to relax, but she says it anyway. And it sounds so real, so much like a physical person standing directly behind my left shoulder, that I actually answer her out loud. I’ll turn around and say, \"What do you want?\" or \"No, I'm doing it right,\" and then... I just freeze. Because I’ll realize the house is completely quiet, my husband is in the other room watching TV, and there is absolutely nobody else in the kitchen with me. \n\nI'll just be standing there holding a mug or a stack of envelopes, realizing nobody else heard it. But I mean, how can nobody else hear it when it’s loud enough for me to reflexively answer back? It just makes my heart pound out of my chest. It’s exhausting enough trying to keep track of the patio servers and the duplicate tickets at work, but to come home and have this woman's voice telling me I'm messing up my count while I'm standing in my own kitchen... it just never really stops, you know? You never really get to just turn your brain off and rest.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back and tell me if there was ever a time when you experienced these kinds of things—like hearing the voice or believing others were plotting against you—for at least one month straight without a break?\n\nPatient: Well, if you mean for a solid month without a single break... yeah, absolutely. Like I was saying earlier, the really intense part of all this, the clear voice and the absolute certainty that the patio servers and that regular customer are plotting against me with the tickets... that's been happening for about four months straight. \n\nAnd when I say four months straight, I mean it hasn't stopped for a single day. Not one day. Even on my days off, which, let's be honest, in the restaurant business a day off isn't really a day off. You're just recovering from the last shift and dreading the next one. You're doing laundry, trying to get the smell of fry oil out of your non-slip shoes, and the whole time, my mind is just racing. I'll be sitting on my couch, trying to watch a show, and I'm just replaying every single interaction I had with the patio servers the night before. Did they look at me weird when I ran their food? Why did they group their tickets like that? \n\nAnd the voice... she doesn't take a day off either. Like I told you, she's there when I'm just trying to make tea in my own kitchen. So, yes, to answer your question, it has definitely been at least a month straight. It's been four months of this constant, exhausting cycle. Four months of feeling like I have to be a detective in my own workplace, analyzing every receipt, every glance, every whisper in the waitstation. You just get so tired, doctor. You get so tired of always being on high alert, always waiting for the other shoe to drop, always waiting for them to finally make their move and get me fired. It's been four months of this, every single day, and I just... I don't know how much longer I can keep this up before I just completely break down in the middle of a dinner rush.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that your symptoms—like hearing the voice and believing others are plotting against you—have been present for at least six months now without a significant break?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\nmanifestation: \"The clearer voices and firm beliefs about coworkers and customers targeting me have been happening for about four months.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, wait, no... I think maybe we are getting the timelines a little crossed here. Like I was just trying to explain a minute ago, the really clear voices? The woman's voice telling me my count is wrong in the kitchen, and that absolute, firm belief that the patio servers and that one regular customer are specifically targeting me with the ticket codes? That specific stuff has only been happening for about four months. 

It hasn't been six months for the voice and the plotting. Six months ago, I was just incredibly stressed out. I was feeling that heavy pressure, you know, feeling like everyone was whispering about me and watching me fail at being the shift lead, but I wasn't actually hearing her voice out loud yet. I wasn't catching them manipulating the receipts yet. All of that really intense, undeniable stuff started exactly four months ago. 

I remember clearly because it was right when corporate made us roll out the new spring menu, and I was already pulling my hair out trying to memorize all the new allergy modifiers for the seasonal salads so I could test the servers on them during pre-shift meetings. And that is exactly when the voice started chiming in and I finally realized what they were actually doing with the duplicate tickets. So, the firm beliefs and the clearer voices, that is a solid four months. The overall feeling of things being wrong and the atmosphere shifting, that is the year and two months. I just want to make sure I'm being completely accurate with you, because I know six months is what you keep asking about, but the really loud, constant stuff where I'm catching them plotting is definitely just a four-month thing.

And I always answer back before my brain catches up and realizes that I live alone and there's absolutely no one else in the apartment. It's this whole subtle code they're using, leaving these little markers on the tickets to build this huge case against me, to make it look like I'm incompetent and messing up the inventory so the manager has an excuse to let me go. \n\nI even started bringing some of the duplicate receipts home in my apron to prove it. I laid them all out on the kitchen table for my husband last week. I was pointing right at the weird spacing and the lowercase letters, showing him exactly how it all connects back to those two servers and that regular customer, but he just looked at me like I was speaking another language. He told me I was overthinking it and said it’s just normal restaurant mess-ups, like somebody's thumb slipped on the screen or the old receipt printer in the kitchen is just glitching again. But you don't just glitch the exact same way three shifts in a row! I know what I'm seeing, and I know they're plotting against me, but because nobody else will actually look at the evidence, they all just think I'm making it up or being paranoid. But I'm not. It's really happening right in front of them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about people trying to get you fired and the signs on the order tickets for at least six months now?\n\nPatient: Six months? Um, no, it hasn't been quite that long. Let me think about it, because, you know, in the restaurant industry, time just kind of blurs together. You're just living shift to shift, lunch rush to dinner rush, and sometimes you don't even know what day of the week it is unless you look at the specials board. \n\nBut if I really sit down and trace it back... the really clear stuff, like hearing that woman's voice so plainly when I'm doing my bank at night, and having those firm, undeniable beliefs about the patio servers and that regular customer targeting me... that really all solidified about four months ago. Yeah, it would be about four months. I spend my whole shift just watching my back, checking every single duplicate receipt that prints out of the kitchen, making sure my cash drawer is absolutely perfect so they can't use it against me, and it's just exhausting. Four months of that kind of stress will really wear a person down, you know? Because you can't just clock out and leave it behind when you know they're probably texting each other about you the second you walk out the door.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any signs or symptoms like these—such as hearing voices or feeling like people are plotting against you—for at least six months now?\n\nPatient: Well, if you mean just the start of it all... like, any signs that something was off or that things were starting to shift, then yes, definitely longer than six months. Like I said before, the really intense stuff, the clear voice and catching them using the ticket codes, that was four months ago. Which, I mean, it sounds like a good thing, like a promotion, right? But in the restaurant industry, being a shift lead just means you do all the manager work for barely any extra hourly pay. You have to handle the voids, the comps, deal with angry tables when the kitchen overcooks a steak, and you have to make sure every single server rolls their silverware and does their sidework before they are allowed to get cut and go home. \n\nAnd that is when everything really started shifting. I was so stressed out about balancing the master drawer and dealing with the nightly deposits that I just felt my whole personality changing.
